People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Hi… thanks for seeing me. I guess I’m here because I’ve been having a hard time shaking this feeling that whenever something goes wrong, it’s my fault. Like I’m not doing enough, or I’m messing things up somehow.

I keep thinking I’m a bad employee, and even a bad homeowner… like I don’t really deserve the things I’ve worked for, or that I’m going to lose them because I’ve somehow failed.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been having these thoughts about not doing enough or messing things up?\n\nPatient: I think… it’s been a little over two years now. A little, maybe, but… honestly not much feels enjoyable the way it used to.

I used to plan brunches with friends, paint in the spare room, go hiking, sign up for networking events—things like that. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

Not really for any long stretch, no. I mean, there have been brief times where I could function a little better, or I seemed more okay from the outside, but looking back the low mood and not really being interested in things has been there almost the whole time since it started.

I keep thinking I’m a bad employee, and even a bad homeowner… like I don’t really deserve the things I’ve worked for, or that I’m going to lose them because I’ve somehow failed.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been having these thoughts about not doing enough or messing things up?\n\nPatient: I think… it’s been a little over two years now. Maybe about two years and three months, if I’m counting right. It started after I bought my townhouse, when the responsibility of keeping up with everything started feeling heavier. I didn’t think it was that bad at first, but it’s kind of stayed with me.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in how much you enjoy activities or things that you used to find pleasurable?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, a little. I don’t want to make it sound like I’ve completely stopped living or anything, but things I used to actually look forward to feel kind of… flat now.\n\nI used to plan brunches with friends, paint in the spare room, go hiking, sign up for networking events—stuff like that. And now, if I do them, it feels more like I’m going through the motions, like they’re tasks I’m supposed to care about rather than things I genuinely enjoy. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or empty most of the time?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I don’t always want to call it “sad,” because I can still get through work and talk to people and everything, but underneath it there’s been this kind of low, empty feeling.\n\nLooking back, it’s been there for most of the time since all this started—same with not really enjoying things. There are little stretches where I feel a bit more functional or more like myself, but they don’t usually last very long. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you still find pleasure in social interactions, like spending time with friends and family?\n\nPatient: Somewhat, I guess… I mean, I still show up, and I don’t want people to think I don’t care about them. But it doesn’t feel the same.\n\nLike, I used to actually plan brunches with friends, or get excited about hiking, painting in the spare room, even going to networking things. Now it’s more like… I’m doing the things I’m supposed to do, and trying to act like I’m into it. It feels more like a task than something I’m really enjoying. Does that answer it?\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods in the past two years where your low mood and empty feeling were significantly worse than they are now?\n\nPatient: I guess… not really in a separate way? It’s more that the depression and that loss of interest have kind of been with me pretty steadily for about two years and one month now.\n\nThere are days or weeks that feel a little heavier than others, but I don’t know if I’d call them a whole separate period. Is that the kind of timeline you’re asking for?\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in or enjoy doing?\n\nPatient: A little, maybe, but… honestly not much feels enjoyable the way it used to.\n\nI used to plan brunches with friends, paint in the spare room, go hiking, sign up for networking events—things like that. And now I can still do them, or at least I try to, but it feels more like I’m checking something off or pretending I care enough. I don’t want to sound dramatic, because I do show up sometimes… it just doesn’t really feel fun anymore. Is that what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Are your feelings of sadness and emptiness something you experience for most of the day, or are they more intermittent?\n\nPatient: Most of the day, I think… I don’t want to overstate it, but it’s kind of been there in the background almost all the time. The low feeling and not really enjoying things have been going on for about two years and one month now.\n\nSome days it’s lighter, but it doesn’t really go away. Is that a clear way to put it?\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods in the past two years where you felt significantly better than you do now?\n\nPatient: Not really for any long stretch, no. I mean, there have been brief times where I could function a little better, or I seemed more okay from the outside, but looking back the low mood and not really being interested in things has been there almost the whole time since it started.\n\nI hope that makes sense… I wouldn’t say I had a real period where I felt significantly better.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of at least two months where you felt your usual self again?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think I’ve had a full two months where I felt like my usual self again.\n\nThere were maybe little stretches where I was functioning better, like getting through work or showing up more, so from the outside it might’ve looked like I was okay. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi… thanks for seeing me. I guess I’m here because I’ve been having a hard time shaking this feeling that whenever something goes wrong, it’s my fault. I keep thinking I’m a bad employee, and even a bad homeowner… like I don’t really deserve the things I’ve worked for, or that I’m going to lose them because I’ve somehow failed.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been having these thoughts about not doing enough or messing things up?\n\nPatient: I think… it’s been a little over two years now. Maybe about two years and three months, if I’m counting right. It started after I bought my townhouse, when the responsibility of keeping up with everything started feeling heavier. I didn’t think it was that bad at first, but it’s kind of stayed with me.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in how much you enjoy activities or things that you used to find pleasurable?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, a little. I don’t want to make it sound like I’ve completely stopped living or anything, but things I used to actually look forward to feel kind of… flat now.\n\nI used to plan brunches with friends, paint in the spare room, go hiking, sign up for networking events—stuff like that. Is that the kind of timeline you’re asking for?\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in or enjoy doing?\n\nPatient: A little, maybe, but… honestly not much feels enjoyable the way it used to.\n\nI used to plan brunches with friends, paint in the spare room, go hiking, sign up for networking events—things like that. And now I can still do them, or at least I try to, but it feels more like I’m checking something off or pretending I care enough. I don’t want to sound dramatic, because I do show up sometimes… it just doesn’t really feel fun anymore. Is that what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Are your feelings of sadness and emptiness something you experience for most of the day, or are they more intermittent?\n\nPatient: Most of the day, I think… I don’t want to overstate it, but it’s kind of been there in the background almost all the time. The low feeling and not really enjoying things have been going on for about two years and one month now.\n\nSome days it’s lighter, but it doesn’t really go away. But looking back, the low mood and the lack of interest were still kind of there most of the time. I wish I could say there was a longer break, but I don’t think there was. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have your feelings of sadness and emptiness been present for most of every day over the past two years?\n\nPatient: Yes… for the most part, yes. I mean, I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but the depression and not really being interested in things have been with me for about two years and one month now.\n\nSome moments are a little easier than others, but it’s been there most days, most of the day. Does that answer it okay?\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any periods of at least two weeks where you didn't feel sad or empty?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not for a full two weeks. Looking back, the low mood and that lack of interest have pretty much been there since this started.\n\nThere were short times where I could function a bit better, like I seemed more okay or kept up with things, but I don’t think I was actually free of feeling sad or empty for two weeks. I hope that’s answering it the right way.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in how bad your feelings of sadness and emptiness have been over the past two years?\n\nPatient: I guess… not huge changes, no. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi… thanks for seeing me. I guess I’m here because I’ve been having a hard time shaking this feeling that whenever something goes wrong, it’s my fault. Like I’m not doing enough, or I’m messing things up somehow.\n\nI know that probably sounds a little dramatic, but it’s been getting to me. I keep thinking I’m a bad employee, and even a bad homeowner… like I don’t really deserve the things I’ve worked for, or that I’m going to lose them because I’ve somehow failed.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been having these thoughts about not doing enough or messing things up?\n\nPatient: I think… it’s been a little over two years now. Maybe about two years and three months, if I’m counting right. It started after I bought my townhouse, when the responsibility of keeping up with everything started feeling heavier. I didn’t think it was that bad at first, but it’s kind of stayed with me.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in how much you enjoy activities or things that you used to find pleasurable?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, a little. I don’t want to make it sound like I’ve completely stopped living or anything, but things I used to actually look forward to feel kind of… flat now.\n\nI used to plan brunches with friends, paint in the spare room, go hiking, sign up for networking events—stuff like that. And now, if I do them, it feels more like I’m going through the motions, like they’re tasks I’m supposed to care about rather than things I genuinely enjoy. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or empty most of the time?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I don’t always want to call it “sad,” because I can still get through work and talk to people and everything, but underneath it there’s been this kind of low, empty feeling.\n\nLooking back, it’s been there for most of the time since all this started—same with not really enjoying things. There are little stretches where I feel a bit more functional or more like myself, but they don’t usually last very long. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you still find pleasure in social interactions, like spending time with friends and family?\n\nPatient: Somewhat, I guess… I mean, I still show up, and I don’t want people to think I don’t care about them. But it doesn’t feel the same.\n\nLike, I used to actually plan brunches with friends, or get excited about hiking, painting in the spare room, even going to networking things. Now it’s more like… I’m doing the things I’m supposed to do, and trying to act like I’m into it. It feels more like a task than something I’m really enjoying. Does that answer it?\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods in the past two years where your low mood and empty feeling were significantly worse than they are now?\n\nPatient: I guess… not really in a separate way? It’s more that the depression and that loss of interest have kind of been with me pretty steadily for about two years and one month now.\n\nThere are days or weeks that feel a little heavier than others, but I don’t know if I’d call them a whole separate period. Is that the kind of timeline you’re asking for?\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in or enjoy doing?\n\nPatient: A little, maybe, but… honestly not much feels enjoyable the way it used to.\n\nI used to plan brunches with friends, paint in the spare room, go hiking, sign up for networking events—things like that. And now I can still do them, or at least I try to, but it feels more like I’m checking something off or pretending I care enough. I don’t want to sound dramatic, because I do show up sometimes… it just doesn’t really feel fun anymore. Is that what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Are your feelings of sadness and emptiness something you experience for most of the day, or are they more intermittent?\n\nPatient: Most of the day, I think… I don’t want to overstate it, but it’s kind of been there in the background almost all the time. The low feeling and not really enjoying things have been going on for about two years and one month now.\n\nSome days it’s lighter, but it doesn’t really go away. Is that a clear way to put it?\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods in the past two years where you felt significantly better than you do now?\n\nPatient: Not really for any long stretch, no. I mean, there have been brief times where I could function a little better, or I seemed more okay from the outside, but looking back the low mood and not really being interested in things has been there almost the whole time since it started.\n\nI hope that makes sense… I wouldn’t say I had a real period where I felt significantly better.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of at least two months where you felt your usual self again?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think I’ve had a full two months where I felt like my usual self again.\n\nThere were maybe little stretches where I was functioning better, like getting through work or showing up more, so from the outside it might’ve looked like I was okay. But looking back, the low mood and the lack of interest were still kind of there most of the time. I wish I could say there was a longer break, but I don’t think there was. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have your feelings of sadness and emptiness been present for most of every day over the past two years?\n\nPatient: Yes… for the most part, yes. I mean, I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but the depression and not really being interested in things have been with me for about two years and one month now.\n\nSome moments are a little easier than others, but it’s been there most days, most of the day. Does that answer it okay?\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any periods of at least two weeks where you didn't feel sad or empty?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not for a full two weeks. Looking back, the low mood and that lack of interest have pretty much been there since this started.\n\nThere were short times where I could function a bit better, like I seemed more okay or kept up with things, but I don’t think I was actually free of feeling sad or empty for two weeks. I hope that’s answering it the right way.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in how bad your feelings of sadness and emptiness have been over the past two years?\n\nPatient: I guess… not huge changes, no. It’s maybe gone up and down a little, like some days I can get through things better and seem more normal.\n\nBut looking back, the sadness and not really being interested in things have been there almost the whole time since it started. There were just brief stretches where I could function a little better, not really times where it was gone. Is that what you mean by changes?\n\nDoctor: Have you had any period of at least two months in the past two years where you felt like you were back to your normal self and your symptoms improved significantly?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think there’s been a full two-month stretch like that. I wish I could say there was.\n\nThere have been little periods where I was getting by better, or I probably looked more like myself from the outside, but the low mood and not really caring about things were still there in the background. So… not really back to normal for two months, no. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n18\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.1, F33.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi… thanks for seeing me. I guess I’m here because I’ve been having a hard time shaking this feeling that whenever something goes wrong, it’s my fault. I keep thinking I’m a bad employee, and even a bad homeowner… like I don’t really deserve the things I’ve worked for, or that I’m going to lose them because I’ve somehow failed.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been having these thoughts about not doing enough or messing things up?\n\nPatient: I think… it’s been a little over two years now. Maybe about two years and three months, if I’m counting right. It started after I bought my townhouse, when the responsibility of keeping up with everything started feeling heavier. I didn’t think it was that bad at first, but it’s kind of stayed with me.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in how much you enjoy activities or things that you used to find pleasurable?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, a little. I don’t want to make it sound like I’ve completely stopped living or anything, but things I used to actually look forward to feel kind of… flat now.\n\nI used to plan brunches with friends, paint in the spare room, go hiking, sign up for networking events—stuff like that. And now, if I do them, it feels more like I’m going through the motions, like they’re tasks I’m supposed to care about rather than things I genuinely enjoy. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or empty most of the time?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I don’t always want to call it “sad,” because I can still get through work and talk to people and everything, but underneath it there’s been this kind of low, empty feeling.\n\nLooking back, it’s been there for most of the time since all this started—same with not really enjoying things. There are little stretches where I feel a bit more functional or more like myself, but they don’t usually last very long. But it doesn’t feel the same.\n\nLike, I used to actually plan brunches with friends, or get excited about hiking, painting in the spare room, even going to networking things. Now it’s more like… I’m doing the things I’m supposed to do, and trying to act like I’m into it. It feels more like a task than something I’m really enjoying. Does that answer it?\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods in the past two years where your low mood and empty feeling were significantly worse than they are now?\n\nPatient: I guess… not really in a separate way? It’s more that the depression and that loss of interest have kind of been with me pretty steadily for about two years and one month now.\n\nThere are days or weeks that feel a little heavier than others, but I don’t know if I’d call them a whole separate period. Is that what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Are your feelings of sadness and emptiness something you experience for most of the day, or are they more intermittent?\n\nPatient: Most of the day, I think… I don’t want to overstate it, but it’s kind of been there in the background almost all the time. The low feeling and not really enjoying things have been going on for about two years and one month now.\n\nSome days it’s lighter, but it doesn’t really go away. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period where you felt significantly better than you do now that lasted for at least a few months?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not for a few months at a time. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"

Yeah… I think so. I mean, I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, but the sadness and not really enjoying things have been there for about two years and maybe a month now.
